This technical whitepaper outlines the Nexus-AI ecosystem, a comprehensive architectural framework designed to transition modern enterprises from fragmented AI tools to a unified operational central nervous system. The text describes a multi-layered stack ranging from high-performance hardware—such as AI-optimized silicon and GPU clusters—to an intelligent orchestration layer that simplifies model routing and data integration through standardized protocols. A central theme is the consolidation of disparate resources into a single logical interface, which enhances scalability, security, and cost-management via real-time observability and military-grade guardrails. Ultimately, the document serves as a roadmap for systems engineers to evolve from manual oversight toward a future of autonomous AI agents capable of independent reasoning and cross-departmental collaboration.

Architectural Orchestration of the Nexus-AI Ecosystem: A Technical Whitepaper for Systems Administrators and Operations Engineers
The contemporary enterprise landscape is undergoing a fundamental transition from fragmented, isolated artificial intelligence implementations to a unified, cohesive operational framework. This evolution is encapsulated in the concept of the "Nexus," a multifaceted ecosystem of hardware, routing protocols, and cognitive engines that collectively serve as the central nervous system for modern digital organizations. For systems administrators and technical operations engineers, managing this ecosystem requires a nuanced understanding of how disparate technologies—ranging from 800G silicon photonics to decentralized machine learning operations—converge into a single logical entity. This report provides an exhaustive technical analysis of the various platforms bearing the Nexus designation, elucidating their architectural foundations, operational requirements, and the strategic implications of their deployment within a high-scale enterprise environment.
The Convergence of Intelligence: An Executive Summary for Systems Engineering
The term "Nexus-AI" does not describe a single, monolithic product but rather a paradigm of interconnected systems designed to solve the challenges of accessibility, interoperability, and scalability in the artificial intelligence lifecycle.[1, 2] At the hardware level, it represents high-performance networking fabrics optimized for the massive "east-west" traffic characteristic of distributed GPU clusters. At the orchestration layer, it manifests as intelligent routers that unify Large Language Model (LLM) providers and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers behind a single, high-performance endpoint. Finally, at the application and governance layers, it provides the framework for autonomous agents that can reason, collaborate, and execute decisions with minimal human intervention.[3, 4, 5]
The primary objective for technical administrators is to eliminate the inefficiencies caused by the dispersion of AI tools across diverse platforms.[1] By consolidating these disparate resources into a singular interface, the Nexus architecture addresses the existing challenges of accessibility and interoperability, fostering an environment where developers, researchers, and business units can harness AI potential in a cohesive and efficient manner.[1] This consolidation is supported by military-grade security, high-availability guarantees of 99.99% uptime, and a transition toward autonomous operations that learn and act without constant human prompting.[3, 6]
Physical Layer Infrastructure: Silicon, Fabric, and High-Performance Compute
The foundation of any AI ecosystem is the physical infrastructure capable of supporting the computational and networking demands of modern models. In the Nexus framework, this foundation is primarily defined by high-performance data center architectures that integrate custom silicon with specialized AI accelerators.
Silicon One and the Evolution of AI Networking
Traditional data center networks were not built for the unique demands of AI workloads, which require high-performance, secure, and adaptable infrastructure to manage constantly changing workloads.[5] The Cisco Nexus portfolio, particularly those switches powered by Cisco Silicon One, addresses this by providing a unified operating model that spans on-premises and cloud environments.[5] Silicon One represents a shift toward a programmable, AI-optimized architecture with a total capacity of up to 51.2 Tbps, enabling the high-density switching required for modern clusters.[5]
For sysops, the Cisco N9100 Series represents a critical hardware milestone. These switches incorporate NVIDIA Spectrum-X Ethernet switch silicon, unified under the Nexus operating model to provide seamless integration with the Nexus Dashboard.[5] This allows operators a choice between Cisco’s NX-OS for enterprise-grade reliability or SONiC for organizations preferring open networking standards with containerized functions and API support.[5]
GPU Cluster Reference Architectures and Server Specifications
The deployment of large-scale AI models necessitates a structured approach to hardware configuration. The Nexus architecture utilizes validated reference architectures to ensure that compute, storage, and networking work in concert.

The UCS C885A M8 server configuration is specifically engineered for Large Language Model (LLM) training, fine-tuning, and large model inferencing. The integration of high-speed NVLink interconnects between the eight GPUs within the server allows for massive internal bandwidth, while the BlueField-3 DPUs handle the external networking at 400 GbE, ensuring that the network does not become a bottleneck during data-intensive operations.[5]
Scalability and Multi-Tenancy in AI Fabrics
For service providers and large enterprises, the ability to scale and isolate workloads is paramount. The Cisco Nexus 9000 Cloud Reference Architecture is designed to scale from clusters of 8,000 GPUs up to 16,000 GPUs.[5] This scale is achieved through the use of redundant leaf-spine topologies and parallel planes that deliver up to 128 Tbps of aggregate storage bandwidth.[5]
Technical administrators manage multi-tenancy through Virtual Routing and Forwarding (VRF), which isolates traffic for individual tenants and administrative functions. The architecture typically defines management VRFs for out-of-band management (BMC and host ports) and tenant-specific VRFs to isolate "backend" east-west compute traffic from "frontend" storage and management traffic.[5] This level of isolation is critical for security and performance consistency in shared environments.
Logical Layer Orchestration: The Nexus Enterprise AI Router
Above the physical infrastructure sits the orchestration layer, which manages how requests are routed to various AI models and tools. The Nexus Enterprise AI Router, developed by Grafbase, represents the state-of-the-art in this domain, providing a unified endpoint for all AI operations.[4]
Rust-Powered High-Performance Routing
Efficiency at the routing layer is essential to minimize the total latency of AI interactions. The Nexus Enterprise router is built with Rust, a systems programming language chosen for its memory safety and high-performance characteristics.[4] This allows the router to handle thousands of concurrent requests with minimal overhead. The unified routing layer serves as a single intelligent API endpoint, simplifying integration at scale by allowing developers to connect to any MCP server or LLM without rewriting application code.[4]
The technical complexity of the router is hidden behind a consistent API interface that supports major providers such as OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and AWS Bedrock. For sysops, this means that swapping a model provider is a configuration change rather than a code change, enabling faster time-to-market and easier experimentation with new models.[4]
Model Context Protocol (MCP) and Tool Aggregation
A key innovation in the Nexus orchestration layer is the support for the Model Context Protocol (MCP). This protocol allows AI models to securely interact with external tools and services, such as local files, databases, or third-party APIs.[2] The Nexus router aggregates these MCP servers via multiple protocols, including:
• STDIO: Standard input/output for local process interaction.
• SSE (Server-Sent Events): For real-time data streaming from tool servers.
• HTTP: For standard web-based service calls.[4]
By providing namespaced tool aggregation and intelligent connection caching, Nexus maintains high performance while enforcing strict governance over which tools an AI agent can access. This prevents unauthorized tool usage and ensures that sensitive data sources remain protected.
Observability and Financial Governance
One of the most significant challenges for technical admins in the AI era is the management of operational costs and token spend. Nexus Enterprise provides real-time observability through dashboards that track metrics, tracing, and health checks across all agents and models.[4]
Sysops can define "routable budgets" and rate limits per team or per provider. This proactive spend control is a critical technical requirement for enterprise deployments, as it prevents "budget shock" caused by runaway AI processes or unoptimized prompts. The platform provides granular visibility into token consumption, allowing administrators to optimize model selection based on cost-to-performance ratios.[4]
The MLOps Lifecycle: TensorOpera and Cross-Cloud Model Serving
The Nexus ecosystem extends into the realm of MLOps through platforms like TensorOpera AI (formerly FEDML Nexus AI), which provides the tools needed to train, deploy, and scale models across geo-distributed environments.[7]
The Five-Layer Serving Architecture
The TensorOpera platform utilizes a structured 5-layer architecture designed for high-performance model serving and management:
1. Layer 0 (Deployment and Inference Endpoint): This layer provides the HTTPS API and manages model customization (fine-tuning), scalability, and scheduling. It also handles logging, monitoring, and compliance (SOC2).[7]
2. Layer 1 (Launch Scheduler): A specialized component that collaborates with the MLOps platform to handle the deployment workflow on specific GPU devices, ensuring that serving code and configurations are correctly applied.[7]
3. Layer 2 (Serving Framework): A managed framework responsible for scalability and observability. it loads the serving engine and the user-level serving code.[7]
4. Layer 3 (Model Definition and Inference APIs): This layer allows developers to define the model architecture, the inference engine, and the schema of the related APIs.[7]
5. Layer 4 (Inference Engine and Hardware): The execution layer where optimizations occur. TensorOpera introduces ScaleLLM, an in-house inference library optimized for memory efficiency and throughput.[7]
ScaleLLM: Optimizing Inference for Distributed Environments
For tech-admins, ScaleLLM represents a significant advancement in resource optimization. It is a serverless, memory-efficient engine that utilizes weight quantization, KV cache quantization, and fast attention mechanisms to deliver high performance on both enterprise and consumer-grade GPUs.[7]
Technical benchmarks demonstrate that ScaleLLM can host a LLaMA-2-13B-chat model on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 with latency nearly 2x lower than vLLM on a high-end A100.[7] This efficiency allows for the migration of complex models to lower-cost hardware, such as single L4 or T4 GPUs, while maintaining sub-second time-to-first-token performance.[7]
Geo-Distributed Endpoints and Fault Tolerance
A unique technical feature of the TensorOpera Nexus platform is the ability for a single endpoint to span multiple geo-distributed GPU clouds.[7, 8] This architecture provides several systemic advantages:
• Reliability: By spreading resources across multiple clouds, the system ensures that if one provider experiences downtime, the service remains available via other providers.[7, 9]
• Performance: Proximity to users can be optimized by running endpoints on clouds geographically closer to the request origin.[7]
• Risk Management: Multi-cloud distribution helps mitigate risks associated with regional policy regulations and data sovereignty requirements.[7]
• Cost Management: Sysops can leverage decentralized networks of consumer-grade GPUs, which are claimed to be up to 20 times more economical than traditional cloud instances for certain workloads.[7]
Admins can bind on-premise servers to this decentralized cloud using a simple CLI command (fedml login <API key>), allowing for a hybrid approach that combines local hardware with global scale.[7, 9]
Intelligent Data Querying and Verifiability: The Nuklai Nexus Engine
A critical bottleneck in enterprise AI is the "hallucination" problem and the difficulty of verifying AI-generated answers. Nuklai’s Nexus addresses this by functioning as a natural language query engine that connects directly to the organization's data sources.[10]
Just-in-Time Data Conversion
Unlike a standard chatbot, which relies on its training data, Nuklai Nexus acts as a translation layer between human language and data systems. It treats disparate data sources—databases, spreadsheets, APIs, and cloud services—as a unified landscape.[10] The engine uses "just-in-time conversion" to query non-SQL sources, such as files and APIs, as if they were standard relational tables.[10]
For sysops, this eliminates the need for complex ETL processes just to make data accessible to an AI. Nexus understands the data where it lives, figures out the necessary query logic, and returns a natural language response accompanied by the underlying SQL or tool calls used to generate it.[10] This transparency is vital for auditing and technical troubleshooting.
Helix Blockchain and the Data Economy
The verifiability of Nuklai Nexus is further enhanced through its integration with Helix, a Cosmos-based blockchain designed for the "on-chain data economy".[10] Helix handles access control, trustless monetization, and verification.
Every time Nexus accesses data, the transaction is logged on-chain. This ensures that:
1. Data providers are accurately compensated for their content.
2. Every AI answer can be traced back to its specific source.
3. Access is restricted to authorized parties through decentralized identity mechanisms.[10]
This blockchain integration provides a level of data provenance that is often missing from traditional enterprise AI platforms, making it highly suitable for industries with strict regulatory requirements.
Advanced Pattern Recognition and Predictive Analytics: White Pearl Nexus AI
In operational contexts like municipal management or large-scale financial services, the Nexus AI platform (developed by White Pearl Technology Group) provides specialized engines for pattern recognition and predictive forecasting.[6]
Core Analytical Capabilities
The White Pearl Nexus platform is built on an enterprise-ready architecture designed for high-volume transaction loads, such as those found in millions of municipal transactions.[6] The Predictive Analytics Engine utilizes several statistical and machine learning methodologies:
• Time Series Forecasting: Essential for energy load forecasting and trend analysis.[6]
• Classification Models: Used for decision support and categorical outcomes like fraud detection.[6]
• Regression Analysis: Applied to continuous variable prediction and cost optimization.[6]
• Survival Analysis: Employed for equipment lifecycle management and "time-to-event" predictions.[6]
These models are often deployed as part of an ensemble approach, combining multiple algorithms to achieve higher accuracy and lower false-positive rates.[6]
Military-Grade Security and High Availability
The White Pearl implementation (specifically the OMNI-NEXUS deployment) emphasizes "military-grade" security to protect sensitive financial and citizen data.[6] This includes:
• NEXUS CORE: An integration foundation with a 99.99% uptime guarantee.[6]
• Micro-transaction Monitoring: A technique for detecting small-scale manipulation attempts that might be missed by broader fraud detection systems.[6]
• Integrated Case Management: A complete workflow for investigating and resolving anomalies identified by the pattern recognition algorithms.[6]
From a sysops perspective, the platform is designed to integrate seamlessly with existing SAP ERP systems, ensuring that AI-driven insights are immediately actionable within the organization's primary system of record.[6]
The Shift to Autonomous Operations: Enterprise Cognitive Nexus
A forward-looking perspective on the Nexus architecture, as presented in Visionet’s "Enterprise Cognitive Nexus," suggests a transition from supervised AI to autonomous AI agents.[3]
The Evolution of Enterprise Intelligence
The evolution of enterprise AI is categorized into four waves:
1. Rules-based Automation: Linear logic for repetitive processes.
2. Predictive Models: Narrow AI for scoring and classification.
3. Generative AI: Productivity boosters reliant on human-in-the-loop prompting.
4. Autonomous AI Agents: Virtual co-workers that understand intent, apply context, and execute decisions without constant intervention.[3]
The Cognitive Nexus framework represents the fourth wave, where agents collaborate across departments—from IT and finance to marketing—interacting with APIs and other agents to achieve high-level business goals.[3]
Codifying Tribal Knowledge
For autonomous agents to function, business knowledge must be made machine-ready. This involves codifying "tacit and tribal knowledge" using ontologies, taxonomies, and business rule engines.[3] The Cognitive Nexus architecture consists of five interconnected components:
• Codified Intelligence: Structured business knowledge.
• Autonomous Agents: Executional units that drive outcomes.
• Enterprise Context: Deeply embedded industry-specific models.
• Cognitive Orchestration: A layer that manages agent-human collaboration.
• Responsible AI Guardrails: Ensuring transparency, explainability, and safety.[3]
This framework shifts the AI paradigm from isolated use cases to orchestrated intelligence at scale, requiring tech-admins to manage the "knowledge graph" of the company as a critical piece of infrastructure.[3]
Security, Governance, and Risk Management Frameworks
The deployment of Nexus-AI necessitates a robust security and governance framework to mitigate the unique risks associated with generative AI and autonomous agents.
Unified Governance and IAM
Nexus Enterprise AI routers and platforms like NexusTek emphasize centralized security and observability. Administrators have access to built-in authentication and authorization, including OAuth2 protection, TLS verification, and namespaced tool access.[4, 11]

Furthermore, the IBM AI Atlas Nexus provides tooling to help govern foundation models by turning abstract risk definitions into actionable workflows.[12] This includes an ontology that combines the AI risk view (taxonomies, mitigations) with the AI model view (evaluations, performance metrics) into a coherent schema.[12]
AI-Driven Cybersecurity and Threat Detection
Proofpoint’s Nexus AI framework represents a specialized security application of the Nexus concept. It combines Natural Language Processing (NLP), generative AI, and behavioral analytics to counter evasive threats such as Business Email Compromise (BEC) and malware-laden communications.[13, 14]
This system applies AI to inspect content, identify sensitive data, and analyze user behavior to distinguish between legitimate activity and high-risk exfiltration attempts.[14] For systems admins, this means security is no longer just about firewalls and antivirus signatures but about "behavioral intelligence" that can adapt to the speed of AI-powered attacks.
Compliance and Regulatory Alignment
For organizations in regulated industries, Nexus provides specific tools for compliance. The "Document Inspector" module, for instance, performs automated checks against 600+ UK CCI rules, flagging gaps and inconsistencies before documents are submitted to regulators.[15] In the educational sector, Nexus is built to meet FERPA standards, ensuring that student and staff information is handled with appropriate care and security protocols.[16]
Deployment Roadmaps and Technical Implementation
The successful integration of the Nexus-AI platform requires a phased approach to manage the transition from existing legacy systems to an AI-native operational model.
The Four-Phase Deployment Roadmap (Insight NEXUS)
A typical deployment strategy for the Nexus platform follows a clear trajectory:
1. NEXUS Foundation: Establishing a secure starting point through AI readiness assessments, security reviews, and the deployment of the core platform with access to 40+ language models in a private environment.[17]
2. NEXUS Velocity: Building internal knowledge and driving adoption through training modules, workshops, and ongoing consulting. This phase focuses on team empowerment and confidence-building.[17]
3. NEXUS Secure Core: Unifying tools in one platform with centralized operations. This includes setting up granular access controls, multi-factor authentication, and analytics dashboards for full oversight.[17]
4. NEXUS Amplify: Deploying advanced AI agents tailored to specific business functions like customer service, finance, and operations. These "digital workers" run continuously to unlock new organizational capacity.[17]
Sysops and Managed Operations
For organizations lacking internal specialized talent, Managed AI Services (such as those from NexusTek) provide an alternative.[11] This includes "Managed AI Operations" (handling GPU infrastructure) and a "Secure AI Platform" that ensures model access is safe and governable.[11] These services typically offer:
• 01. ADVISE: Evaluating current data, infrastructure, and AI capabilities.
• AI Readiness Assessment: Reviewing compliance, data classification, and governance.[11]
• Ongoing Monitoring: Continuously managing systems to drive performance and value realization.
Kubernetes and Containerized Deployment
Most modern Nexus components are designed to be deployed using container orchestration platforms like Google Kubernetes Engine (GKE).[18] This allows for the scalability and flexibility required to handle varying AI workloads.
Key performance metrics for these deployments include:
• Processing Speed: Up to 10 million records per second in optimized pipelines.[19]
• Latency: As low as 10 milliseconds for real-time decision-making.[19]
• Scalability: Support for up to 1000 nodes in a cluster.[19]
Tech-admins must monitor resource utilization closely, as AI workloads can consume significant CPU and GPU resources. For example, a SuperAGI implementation on Nexus showed an average CPU utilization of 45% and memory utilization of 50% after optimization, compared to higher initial rates.[19]
Technical Synthesis: The Role of the Systems Administrator in a Nexus Environment
In a Nexus-AI environment, the role of the systems administrator evolves from managing servers and networks to orchestrating a complex web of cognitive services and data streams.
Orchestrating the "Single Source of Truth"
A central goal of many Nexus implementations is the creation of a "single source of truth" for AI and advanced analytics.[11] This requires sysops to maintain high-quality data pipelines and architecture. The NexusTek Data Modernization services, for example, bring together data quality, architecture, and pipeline design to create a modern ecosystem built for the AI era.[11]
Administrators must also manage the "AI Agent Building" process. Tools like the Y Combinator-backed "Nexus" platform enable non-technical teams to build agents using natural language, but these agents still depend on the underlying infrastructure (the "Nexus Flow") for reliability and zero-hallucination performance.[20] The sysop's job is to ensure this infrastructure is "bulletproof" while the business teams iterate on the agentic logic.[20]
Performance Tuning and Optimization
Optimizing the Nexus AI environment involves several technical levers:
• Quantization: Reducing the precision of model weights (e.g., from FP16 to INT8 or FP8) to save memory and increase inference speed.[7, 21]
• KV Cache Management: Quantizing the Key-Value cache in transformers to allow for larger batch sizes and longer context windows.[7]
• Deterministic Load Balancing: Using source-based, software-scheduled routing to spreads large tensors across multiple paths in the network, maximizing throughput for converged HPC applications.[8]
The Future of the Nexus: Agentic and Autonomous
The future trajectory of the Nexus platform is toward even greater autonomy. The "Nexus: The AI That Actually Knows What It's Talking About" project by Nuklai highlights a summer 2025 launch that will feature modular, swappable agents using the MCP standard for dynamic multi-agent task handling.[10] These agents will be able to collaborate—one handling extraction, another building charts, a third interpreting results—all without user involvement.[10]
For technical administrators, this means the focus will shift toward "Supervisor Agents" that monitor the interaction lifecycle and enforce reliability through timeout-based interventions and fault-tolerant mechanisms.[22] Systems will need to be resilient to "semantic mismatches" and "resource unavailability," using OWL inferences to prevent inconsistent data from propagating into the knowledge graph.[22]
Strategic Implications and Operational Excellence
The deployment of a Nexus-AI architecture represents a commitment to operational excellence and a proactive stance toward the upcoming wave of autonomous intelligence. By unifying infrastructure, routing, and cognitive logic, organizations can avoid the "fragmented data infrastructure" that has hindered AI adoption in the past.[15]
Key Performance Indicators for Tech-Admins
Systems administrators should evaluate their Nexus implementation against several key performance indicators:
1. Uptime and Availability: Maintaining the 99.99% guarantee for the core integration foundation.[6]
2. Deployment Time: Reducing the time to deploy new AI agents from months to days.[20]
3. Cost Efficiency: Tracking token spend and utilizing decentralized compute resources to achieve up to 20x cost reductions.[7]
4. Security Posture: Implementing Zero Trust architecture and real-time behavioral analysis to detect anomalies.[14, 23]
5. User Self-Sufficiency: Using "Embedded Platform Guidance" to reduce training needs and drive team self-sufficiency.[15]
Future Readiness
As AI capabilities continue to double every 6 to 12 months, the traditional models of automation risk a significant decline in efficacy.[17] The Nexus platform provides a secure roadmap from initial readiness to full-scale agentic deployment, ensuring that organizations can scale and adapt as the technology evolves. The ultimate goal is a "Cognitive Nexus" that allows businesses to think, act, and evolve in real-time, leveraging the full potential of their data and human capital.[3]
For the systems administrator, the Nexus is not just a tool—it is the system of record and the control layer for the future of the enterprise. Mastery of its technical details, from the underlying silicon to the high-level agentic orchestration, is the prerequisite for success in the era of artificial intelligence.

The Enterprise Nexus-AI Framework: A Comprehensive Technical Whitepaper for Systems Operations and Administrative Governance
The rapid proliferation of generative artificial intelligence and agentic workflows has necessitated a paradigm shift in enterprise infrastructure. Traditional silos between development, security, and operations are increasingly inadequate for managing the complexity of non-deterministic model outputs, the security of software supply chains, and the performance requirements of high-density AI workloads. The Nexus-AI ecosystem emerges as a multi-layered architectural solution designed to unify these disparate requirements into a single, observable, and governed framework.[1, 2, 3, 4] This report details the technical specifications, deployment models, and operational protocols required for system administrators and technical operations teams to implement and maintain the Nexus-AI environment.
The Cognitive Infrastructure Layer: Hardware and Physical Design
The physical foundation of the Nexus-AI ecosystem is characterized by the requirement for high-density compute and low-latency networking. For organizations prioritizing data sovereignty and local model execution, the Nexus AI Station provides a specialized on-premises appliance. Unlike standard server hardware, this architecture is optimized for GPU-intensive workloads and high-throughput data movement.[5]
Physical Architecture and Compute Density
The Nexus AI Station is built upon the AMD Ryzen 7 PRO 8845HS processor, utilizing the Zen 4 architecture with 8 cores and 16 threads. This CPU provides a base clock of 3.8 GHz and a boost capability of up to 5.1 GHz, supporting the high-frequency single-thread operations often required for pre-processing large datasets before they are passed to the GPU.[5] The system operates within a Thermal Design Power (TDP) envelope of up to 54W, ensuring that the primary thermal budget of the chassis is reserved for the discrete graphics accelerators.
The system's memory architecture is designed for reliability in mission-critical AI environments. It supports up to 96GB of DDR5-5600 SO-DIMM memory. Critically, the platform supports Error Correction Code (ECC) memory, which is essential for detecting and correcting single-bit errors that could otherwise lead to model weights corruption or silent data degradation.[5]

The expansion capability of the Nexus AI Station centers on a PCIe 4.0 x16 slot (operating at x8 lanes), which provides a 400W power budget for full-size GPUs. This allows for the local execution of large language models (LLMs) with up to 70 billion parameters, effectively eliminating the latency and privacy concerns associated with cloud-based inference.[5]
Network Fabric and Fabric Control
In larger enterprise deployments, the Nexus-AI framework integrates with high-performance networking architectures such as the Cisco Nexus HyperFabric. This cluster-based approach marries Nexus 9000 data center switches with NVIDIA H200 Tensor Core GPUs and BlueField-3 DPUs.[6, 7]
For SysOps, the implementation of Remote Direct Memory Access over Converged Ethernet (RoCEv2) is a critical technical requirement. RoCEv2 allows for the direct transfer of data between the memory of two GPU-equipped nodes without involving the server's CPU or OS kernel, significantly reducing the overhead of distributed training and large-scale inference.[7] The Cisco Nexus 9000 switches support up to 25.6Tbps of bandwidth per ASIC, utilizing Explicit Congestion Notification (ECN) to manage the micro-bursts typical of AI training traffic.[7]
The Connectivity Layer: AI Routing and Gateway Management
The Nexus-AI framework utilizes a specialized routing layer to abstract the complexities of multiple LLM providers and tool-use protocols. The Grafbase Nexus router serves as this intelligent gateway, built using Rust to ensure high-concurrency handling with minimal latency overhead.[3, 8]
Model Context Protocol (MCP) Integration
Central to the routing capability is the support for the Model Context Protocol. MCP defines a standardized interaction between the AI agent (Host) and the tools it consumes (Servers). Nexus acts as an aggregator, providing a single endpoint that namespaces tools from multiple MCP servers to prevent naming collisions.[2, 9]
Protocol Feature
Technical Implementation
Administrative Benefit
Unified Endpoint
Single REST/gRPC path for all LLMs
Simplified firewall and proxy configuration [2, 3]
Namespace Isolation
Automatic prefixing (e.g., github__, jira__)
Conflict-free integration of 4,000+ plugins [2, 9]
Connection Caching
Persistent TCP/TLS sessions to providers
Reduction in time-to-first-token (TTFT) metrics [3]
Health Monitoring
/health and /metrics endpoints
Real-time visibility into upstream provider availability [2, 8]
The router supports multiple communication protocols for downstream MCP servers, including standard input/output (STDIO) for local subprocesses, Server-Sent Events (SSE), and streamable HTTP.[2, 8] This flexibility allows administrators to deploy tool servers either as local sidecars or as distributed microservices.
Configuration and Environment Governance
The Nexus router is managed via a nexus.toml configuration file. This file allows for fine-grained control over which models and protocols are exposed to the agentic layer.
[server]
listen_address = "0.0.0.0:8000"

[llm.providers.openai]
type = "openai"
api_key = "{{ env.OPENAI_API_KEY }}"
models = ["gpt-4o", "gpt-4o-mini"]

[mcp.servers.github]
cmd = ["npx", "-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"]
env = { GITHUB_TOKEN = "{{ env.GITHUB_TOKEN }}" }
Administrators can utilize environment variable substitution to ensure that sensitive API keys are never stored in plain text within the configuration file.[2, 8] Furthermore, the system supports header insertion for MCP servers, enabling the injection of static authentication tokens for internal APIs that do not natively support OAuth2.[8]
The Security and Governance Layer: Zero Trust for AI Agents
As AI agents move from advisory roles to autonomous execution, the risk of unauthorized tool calls increases. Nexus-AI addresses this through a Zero Trust proxy architecture that treats every agentic request as a discrete authorization decision.[10, 11]
Identity Governance and Access Proxying
The Nexus Security Proxy, often integrated via SecurEnds or similar IGA platforms, evaluates every tool call against three potential outcomes: Allow, Deny, or Transform.[10] This allows SysOps to enforce granular policies on what an agent can do, even if the model itself is "prompted" to exceed its authority.
1. Payload-Aware Enforcement: Unlike traditional RBAC which only checks if a user can access a service, Nexus uses Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) to inspect the content of the request. For example, an agent might be allowed to call a "Financial Transfer" tool but only for amounts under $5,000.[10]
2. Identity and Context: The proxy validates the identity of both the user and the specific agent instance using OAuth2 and JWT validation. It polls JWKS (JSON Web Key Sets) endpoints at configurable intervals to ensure token integrity.[2, 8]
3. Audit Visibility: Every action—whether allowed or denied—is logged with full context, including the raw JSON-RPC payload and the upstream model's reasoning. These logs are critical for meeting SOC 2 and ISO 27001 compliance standards.[3, 10, 12]
Security Control
Mechanism
Regulatory Alignment
OAuth2 / SSO
Integration with Microsoft Entra ID / Google Workspace
NIST 800-53, ISO 27001 [10, 11]
AES-256 Encryption
Encryption at rest for all vector metadata

OWASP Top 10 for LLMs [11]
Behavioral Malware Defense
Within the software supply chain, Nexus-AI (via the Sonatype Nexus One platform) utilizes machine learning to detect "malicious intent" in open-source components before they are ingested by developers or AI agents. This behavioral analysis goes beyond signature-based detection, identifying patterns such as unexpected file system exfiltration or the creation of mutexes from non-PE files.[1, 14] For SysOps, this provides a "Repository Firewall" that automatically blocks compromised packages from entering the CI/CD pipeline.[1]
The Application and DevOps Layer: Agentic DevSecOps
Nexus-AI redefines the software development lifecycle by introducing agentic automation into dependency management and vulnerability remediation. This is particularly relevant for managing Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs), which have become a legal requirement in many jurisdictions.[1, 15]
Automated Remediation and Dependency Management
The Nexus One platform acts as the system of record for software artifacts. It achieves a 30% faster mean time to remediate (MTTR) by using agentic automation to identify vulnerable dependencies and automatically suggesting (or applying) version upgrades that comply with enterprise security policies.[1]
• Continuous Monitoring: The platform monitors 270 million open-source components, identifying 70% more vulnerabilities than standard public databases.[1]
• Agentic Version Selection: When a vulnerability is detected, the agent analyzes the dependency tree to find a "safe harbor" version—a release that is free of known vulnerabilities and compatible with the existing codebase.[1]
• Policy-as-Code: SysOps can define policies that block builds if they contain components of a certain age, popularity, or license type (e.g., blocking GPL-licensed code in commercial products).[16, 17]
Deployment Models for High-Security Environments
Organizations in defense, nuclear, or critical infrastructure sectors require deployments that are isolated from the public internet. The Sonatype Air-Gapped Environment (SAGE) allows Nexus-AI to function in disconnected networks.[15, 17]
Deployment Model
Technical Stack
Target Environment
SaaS / Cloud

In the air-gapped model, administrators manage a "data bridge" to periodically update the OSS intelligence database without direct external connectivity. This ensures that even isolated teams benefit from real-time vulnerability data while maintaining a absolute security perimeter.[15]
Data Orchestration and Semantic Knowledge Management
A significant challenge for AI implementation is the fragmentation of enterprise data. Nexus-AI solves this through an Enterprise GraphRAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) platform, which constructs a unified knowledge graph from structured and unstructured sources.[18, 19]
GraphRAG and Ontology Generation
Unlike standard RAG, which relies solely on vector similarity, Nexus-AI builds a knowledge graph that understands the relationships between entities. This process is automated through "Nexus Flow," a system that extracts taxonomy and ontology from enterprise documents in minutes.[18, 20]
1. Ingestion: The system connects to SAP ERP, Microsoft 365, and local file systems.[21, 22]
2. Parsing: Specialized parsers (e.g., the ANAFES ESG Data Parser) extract tables and graphics from complex PDFs.[23]
3. Semantic Synthesis: The data is transformed into a "Tree of Logic," where each node represents a business concept and edges represent causal or functional relationships.[24]
4. Retrieval: When a query is made, the agent navigates the graph to find not just the most similar text, but the most contextually relevant chain of data points.[18]
The Virtual Filesystem (VFS) Approach
For SysOps, managing these data connections is simplified by the Nexus VFS architecture. Nexus unifies disparate APIs and SaaS tools into a single path-based API. This means an agent can access a CRM record at /crm/accounts/123 or a cloud storage file at /s3/bucket/data.csv using the same standardized interface.[25]
The VFS includes a two-tier cache layer:
• L1 Cache: In-memory storage for frequently accessed metadata and ReBAC (Relationship-Based Access Control) tuples, ensuring sub-millisecond response times for permission checks.[25]
• L2 Cache: A PostgreSQL-backed persistent store for vector embeddings and historical metadata, allowing for efficient semantic search across all backends.[25]
Operational Maintenance and FinOps
Managing an AI ecosystem requires a focus on both technical health and financial sustainability. Nexus-AI provides built-in tools for monitoring token consumption and performance telemetry.[3, 8]
Token Spend Tracking and Routable Budgets
One of the primary risks for SysOps is the unpredictability of LLM costs. Nexus-AI introduces "routable budgets," which allow administrators to define hard and soft limits on token consumption per team, per project, or per agent.[3]

Telemetry and Observability
Real-time observability is provided through dashboards that track metrics such as request latency, token-per-second (TPS) throughput, and model error rates. By integrating with the Cisco Nexus Dashboard, administrators can correlate AI application performance with underlying network congestion or packet loss.[7] This "single pane of glass" visibility is essential for troubleshooting "hallucinations" that may actually be caused by timeout-induced truncated responses in high-latency network environments.[7, 27]
Domain-Specific Modules and Future Outlook
The Nexus-AI framework is often extended with specialized modules tailored for specific vertical industries. These modules require additional configuration by SysOps to handle unique data formats and compliance requirements.
Financial and Investment Management
The Nexus AI for Investment Managers (provided by FE Fundinfo) focuses on the production of regulatory documents like KIIDs and prospectuses.[28, 29]
• Document Inspector: A technical component that validates fund data against 600+ UKCCI rules, flagging gaps before submission to regulators.[29]
• Data Accuracy: The system monitors over 9 million monthly data points across 100,000 active funds, requiring robust ETL (Extract, Transform, Load) pipelines to maintain a "single source of truth".[28, 29]
Municipal and Smart City Operations
In the public sector, Nexus-AI (via White Pearl Technology Group) handles million of transactions for municipal revenue recovery.[21, 22]
• Integration Foundations: Systems must connect seamlessly with legacy SAP ERP and LIS systems, often requiring custom middleware to bridge modern AI agents with mainframe-era databases.[21, 22]
• Field Management: Real-time GPS tracking and mobile workforce management modules allow AI to route service teams more effectively, predicting infrastructure failures before they occur.[21, 22]
Academic Research and Library Services
The Clarivate Nexus academic assistant bridges AI tools with scholarly indexes. This is implemented via a browser extension that operates within AI environments (like ChatGPT or Claude) to verify references and provide full-text access to library-curated content.[30] Administrators must manage the integration between these extensions and the institution's Learning Management Systems (LMS).[30]
Strategic Continuity and Resilience
The future of the Nexus-AI framework is increasingly tied to the concept of "AI-energy resilience." As AI models require massive amounts of power, SysOps must plan for sustainable ICT-energy networks.[31] This includes the adoption of liquid cooling technologies and the potential integration of small modular reactors (SMRs) for dedicated AI data centers.[31]
Furthermore, the framework is moving toward a decentralized on-chain data economy (e.g., Nuklai/Helix), where data access is logged on a blockchain to provide immutable audit trails and monetization for data providers.[32] This shift will require SysOps to manage blockchain nodes and wallet-based authentication in addition to traditional identity providers.
By unifying hardware performance, protocol routing, zero-trust security, and semantic data management, the Nexus-AI framework provides the essential infrastructure for the next generation of enterprise intelligence. Successful implementation requires a multi-disciplinary approach, combining the skills of network engineers, security analysts, and AI developers under a single operational banner..[1, 3, 10, 12, 18]

T omorrow’ s information business Marjorie M.K. Hlava President, Access Innovations, Inc., 4725 Indian Sc hool Road NE, Suite 100, Albuquerque, NM 87110, USA E-mail: mhlava@accessinn.com Abstract. How do you curate your data today to ensure you can capitalize on it to build a successful information business of tomorrow? Can artiﬁcial intelligence support and enhance human intelligence? What activities might help us build information services that are essentially the foundation of artiﬁcial intelligence in this information community? Where is the best source of user behavioral data? This paper will attempt to answer these questions and more regarding the status of artiﬁcial intelligence today. Keywords: Artiﬁcial intelligence, machine learning, natural language processing, automated language processing, inference engines, text analytics, computational linguistics, word co-occurrence, Bayesian analysis, neural nets, vector spaces, automatic indexing, automatic translation 1. Introduction Artiﬁcial Intelligence has long been the Holy Grail in information and computer science. How can we get the computer to think and act like a human? The computer is so ubiquitous in our lives today it is difﬁcult to remember a time when we weren’t computer-assisted in our daily liv es. So where are we on this journey and how much can we expect to see in our life time? I think of artiﬁcial intelligence (AI) as being on three levels: Narrow , General, and Super-Intelligence. Artiﬁcial Narrow AI is also known as weak AI. It often cov ers a single area or domain. It can, for example, play chess – and very well. Narrow AI is used in service robotics and heavily implemented in machine robotics. It is quite pervasiv e in our lives today . Artiﬁcial General Intelligence, which is also known as strong AI or Human Level AI, involves more reasoning, problem solving, and the creation of complex thoughts based on experience. These are the learning systems that are expected to perform any intellectual task that a human being could perform. At this level, the computer is thought of as being as smart as a human and is the main mantra of AI. Artiﬁcial Super Intelligence is where the computer is smarter than a human, or at least it thinks it is. This is where the wisdom of the machine comes into play and allows the computer to implement solutions based on its knowledge. W e would also expect this system to have the nuances of human interaction represented with appropriate accompanying social skills. Most applications have gotten smaller and the everyday breakthroughs are incredible. In times past, we had to either need to ask a librarian to do a search or go to the reference shelves ourselves to look up an answer. After dinner with my family , we often covered the dining room table with reference books while several of us debated the historical facts supporting a statement. This article is published online with Open Access and distributed under the terms of the Creative Commons Attribution Non-Commercial License. 0167-5265/16/$35.00 © 2016 – IOS Press and the authors.120 M.M.K. Hlava / The data you have. . . T omorrow’s information b usiness 2. Artiﬁcial Intelligence today Now it seems that everyone just grabs their smart phone and asks Siri or her equi valent for answers. W e ask “People” for facts such as how to get to places, where is that restaurant, where is the closest Costco, who was King of England in 1437, and more. This Artiﬁcial Intelligence system, which also translates the spoken query to an online search, parses the query, e xplodes the synonyms, executes the search, translates the answer back to voice that says “here is what I found on the web. ” I can ask it to call my husband, give me mobile directions to a restaurant, and a whole lot more. Sometimes I wonder “what did I do without it?” I had a glove box full of maps and an old phone book in the car to look things up. Where are those maps and that phone book now? At the same time, some things are beginning to look different in our world today . Moore’ s law was busted in 2015 for the ﬁrst time and technology is changing. Moore’s La w said that technology change doubled every eighteen months. Now the hardware changes are not mo ving as quickly, but the softw are implementations are diverse and exploding. It often seems that games and gamiﬁcation are leading the way . It’s not just capacity anymore that makes the dif ference. It has been a meteoritic curve. Other changes due to the breath of capability and hardware support have markedly changed as well. In February 2016, a one hundred year old theory was ﬁnally factually veriﬁed. Gravitational wa ves theorized by Einstein in 1919 are now an established fact. Other fun and amazing things from science ﬁction are regular working applications now , like the Star Trek T ricorder, a version of which can be used now in the ﬁeld by geologists. Our cell phones are no longer just mobile devices for making calls. They are smaller and much more capable. Mine is a phone, a computer, and a ﬂashlight and holds my novels for a long trip! I can watch a movie, buy a latte, hail a ride, deposit checks, start the washing machine or check the security on my house. If offers so much more than Ma Bell offered using that black land line de vice only a few years ago. AI is now used in warfare to provide facial recognition, programmable weaponized or reconnaissance drones, or mobile assassins. Robots have changed from block-headed TV screens on mechanical legs to storm troopers with Plexiglas monitors that acquire information. A look back at Roman siege craft and works shows wooden structures, catapults, and treb uchets near- ing castles with ramparts, towers, and moats. Now there are satellites transmitting ov er radio as well as wireless unmanned aerial vehicles (UA Vs) and other client systems that can pinpoint information in real time. These systems have been moved to the pri vate and commercial sectors as well, providing infor- mation on weather, trafﬁc patterns, utility repair , event management, and sending packages via Amazon and FedEx at incredible speeds across the world. One of the much-heralded recent AI events was the appearance of IBM W atson on the TV game show Jeopardy. W atson looks like a computer and beat the other contestants on Jeopardy. The stage view showed two human contestants and a computer screen for W atson. In fact, behind the curtain it looked quite different – the second room needed for W atson during the Jeopardy show held 53 IBM Mainframe computers! The real IBM W atson needed to perform on Jeopardy included: • 90 IBM Power 750 Express Servers @ $34,500 each; • 8 core processers per machine; • Each with 4 sets of CPU’s; • 32 processors per box; • T otal of 2880 CPU’s; M.M.K. T omorrow’s information b usiness 121 • Three second response time; • T otal processing power 80 teraﬂops; • 1 trillion apps per second; • $3 million just for the hardware. In addition, there was the building of the W atson software, which included many databases, dictionar- ies, voice translation, speed of lookup and all the collateral parts. All of the items I have mentioned so far and which I believ e the reader will be well acquainted with are level one AI. What does a real reasoning system look like? 3. Curr ent AI systems Remember the computer Hal in 2001 Space Odyssey? He said to the remaining human, “Sorry Dave, I can’t do that. ” The computer, Hal, had reasoned that in fact it was superior to the human, and needed to survive without Dav e the human. Another example is the recently revitalized Stepford Wi ves. They were not really human, but rather made to seem human and be the perfect wives for a group of men. Until a real human woman came into their midst many of the men had no idea that their perfect females were in fact computer-driven. So how did we get from being thinking sentient creatures like the chimpanzee, to humans, to the ancient Roman trebuchet, to the current use of computers and artiﬁcial intelligence? Where are we on that journey? And more importantly to us today , what about the application of AI in information services and for publishers? How can we leverage that technology for better sales, discov ery, and deli very of our products and services? When I think of AI today I see the conﬂuence of several forces. They come together to form a ﬁeld that we can harness in many ways: • Artiﬁcial Intelligence; • Computational Linguistics; • Automated Language Processing; • Natural Language Processing; • Co-occurrence; • Inference Engines; • Bayesian Analysis; • Neural Nets; • V ector Spaces; • T ext Analytics; • Automatic Indexing; • Automatic Translation. All of these, just like the IBM W atson, work more accurately with a dictionary or taxonomy in use behind the scenes. One example of an AI Application for publishing is in detecting SciGen or artiﬁcially-generated tech- nical papers. Automatically generated “professional” papers have been around, embarrassingly , for a while. MIT students developed the system in 2005 and since then it has been broadly used to create 122 M.M.K. Hlava / The data you have. . . T omorrow’s information b usiness papers in scientiﬁc disciplines. W e built a program to detect these papers either in a large corpus or on author submission. W e have found them everywhere we look ed. These automatically-generated papers give peer revie w a black eye: they are easy to create, and really hard to detect. Although there is no coherent story line, the dictionary-generated papers use big words strung together so they look like real scientiﬁc papers. A peer reviewer reading such a paper after a long workday will often just pass the paper along as acceptable since it is totally obscure, but sounds really good. Here is a sample paper segment from a SciGen Abstract. “Many theorists would agree that, had it not been for DNS, the simulation of randomized algo- rithms might never have occurred. In this position paper , we demonstrate the investigation of ex- pert systems. In our resear ch, we demonstrate not only that gigabit switches can be made modu- lar , read-write , and constant-time, but that the same is true for online algorithms. W e conﬁrmed that while the seminal encrypted algorithm for the evaluation of RPCs by T akahashi is impossi- ble, Internet QoS and operating systems can interfere to r ealize this ambition. Our framework for evaluating hierarc hical databases is daringly encouraging. Similarly, we also intr oduced an anal- ysis of the partition table. We disconﬁrmed that although simulated annealing can be made secur e, multimodal, and pervasive, rasterization can be made ambimorphic, wireless, and signed .” Look at the last, underlined sentence. T otal gibberish. How do you detect these things? W e do it using a combination of AI techniques in order to ﬁnd the patterns. W e have av ailable many different techniques and systems to enhance our publications, deliver them effectively , and capture original data to work on. These include, but are not limited to, logical tech- niques, digital reasoning, end-user attributes, procedural reasoning, machine reasoning, context attribute reasoning systems, automated reasoning, data attributes, conclusion generation, expert systems, gesture and emotion recognition, sentiment analysis, and I could go on, but they are all variations on a theme. They all depend on available kno wledge. They do not create knowledge or wisdom. Another area that has engaged in AI is medical applications. Medical coding and billing is one of them. The Center for Medical Systems ﬁnally won Congressional approval to implement the ICD-10 coding system in the US. The rest of the world adopted it years ago. The code set went from seventeen thousand codes to over one hundred and sev enty-eight thousand codes (including hospital codes). The industry feared their own Y2K Event. The huge number of codes and the implementation of them could have had a horrendous effect on the cash ﬂo w of medical systems already burdened by heavy regulation and oversight. Some of the AI used in medicine is to provide help in the medical diagnosis itself. The patient encounter is the baseline for the information available via the Electronic Medical Record (see Figs 1 and 2 ). 4. Okay it looks good, but can you afford it? Many of the systems that you see today have wonderful demos that start with the classiﬁcation and mapping of application attributes. These are beautiful, but usually handcrafted, so they are not so much an artiﬁcial intelligence application, but rather a custom built imitation of one. There are computationally-intensive demos that are expensi ve to create, must be further tailored to your data, and create system lifelong maintenance with handcuffs to the vendor who built the system for you.M.M.K. Hlava / The data you have. . . T omorrow’s information b usiness 123 Fig. 1. IntegraCoder electronic health care analysis. Fig. 2. SciGen detector probability distribution plot. 5. Enhancing content What do you have that you can use now? AI today is algorithms – based on logic. It cannot make inferences without semantic control. Look at how an ontology statement might work: <DOI1234> has topic <horses> <DOI1235> has topic <horse> <DOI1236> has topic <equines> These are completely different sets and if they are not connected, they are statistically and therefore informationally worthless. The concepts themselves, represented by these words, are what we need to make into something we can interpret. Or, a machine can interpret it for us. The concepts are represented 124 M.M.K. Hlava / The data you have. . . T omorrow’s information b usiness in many ways: pictures, videos, words. W e use all of them and know what we mean, but the computer is very literal in its interpretation. The computer needs content and synonyms to make the proper judgment and return contextually-appropriate results in a search. The metadata which is kept or separated by us into several elements like journal name, issue, date, DOI, paper, authors, author afﬁliations, date, price, etc., needs to be g athered into discrete ﬁelds or XML elements. Most publishers have done this. Then it is good to know the topics or subjects of each object (article, for example, or technical paper). In order to count it, we have to computer index, or sort it, and then it becomes part of the information process. With those basic elements in place we can add logic statements, like triples, which can then feed the appropriate algorithms for everything from search to advanced analytics. But you can’ t mess up the units, or the analytics will be rotten. Y ou can, howev er, make the most of the data collected that is your content by ensuring that the core data is correct and consistently managed. Other things added semantically such as concepts, topics, and taxonomies, make that data come alive in the AI sense. It allo ws inferences, ﬁnding meaning, and trends in the data that you already own. Content has to go through the semantic “equalizer, ” or a controlled list of topics or taxonomy terms (a.k.a. ontology, thesaurus, subject headings, ke ywords, descriptors) which are needed to control the data to make it statistically accurate. Using as many synonyms as you can ﬁnd will also help leverage the data and increase disco very options for the users. Indexing rules need to be considered carefully by ensuring that the synonyms are applied, otherwise bubbles or groupings of like items are not covering the same material. For e xample, horse, horses, and equines are the same topic to us, but just a string of letters to a computer. Semantic normalization using synonyms and nested terms tells you how many people are really reading on any given subject, and how speciﬁcally the y are interested in it. Y our search logs will also provide the level of speciﬁcity the users of your data expect. Coming back then to the basics for Level 1 AI, we ﬁnd that the building blocks are largely things most publishers already have in varying amounts. They just are not tied together to truly le verage the data in meaningful ways. W e are still too tied to the historic library applications and have not moved to the new frontiers of information science using AI. W e have the concepts – the subject metadata – a vailable for capture via topical offerings, search logs, and web navigations that are already held in classiﬁcation systems and taxonomies or thesauri. Publishers can corral that information to support activities like automatic indexing supported by logical reasoning and accompanying rules created, which will allow tagging or mining the entire corpus published for trends, analysis, search, and discovery . Search and discovery are fully based on lev eraging the word base used to tag the data – optimistically speaking – in a consistent, deep, speciﬁc, system using the term aliases. Once done, other pleasing applications for users like visualization and personalization of the user interaction can happen easily. What about the other levels? Will we reach the Holy Grail? Do we ha ve fully implemented artiﬁcial intelligence? W ell, not yet. Will it happen in my lifetime? I give it a ﬁfty-ﬁfty chance. In the life of my grandchildren? I would say probably so. It is a brave new w orld with many unexplored frontiers for all of us. I look forward to the journey . View... A fraction of the 249 papers (6 papers, 2%) explicitly mention SCIgen and use SCIgen-generated text to exemplify SCIgen usage or output. They either discuss the anatomy of SCIgen papers and their deceptive uses (Bohannon, 2015;Djuric, 2015;Hlava, 2016;Holman, 2009), detect nonsensical papers (Nguyen & Labbé, 2018), or are part of a sting operation to validate a method detecting low-quality conferences (Zhuang et al., 2007). ...Prevalence of nonsensical algorithmically generated papers in the scientific literatureArticle Full-text available
1 May 2021
1 Guillaume Cabanac
2 Cyril Labbé
In 2014 leading publishers withdrew more than 120 nonsensical publications automatically generated with the SCIgen program. Casual observations suggested that similar problematic papers are still published and sold, without follow‐up retractions. No systematic screening has been performed and the prevalence of such nonsensical publications in the scientific literature is unknown. Our contribution is 2‐fold. First, we designed a detector that combs the scientific literature for grammar‐based computer‐generated papers. Applied to SCIgen, it has a 83.6% precision. Second, we performed a scientometric study of the 243 detected SCIgen‐papers from 19 publishers. We estimate the prevalence of SCIgen‐papers to be 75 per million papers in Information and Computing Sciences. Only 19% of the 243 problematic papers were dealt with: formal retraction (12) or silent removal (34). Publishers still serve and sometimes sell the remaining 197 papers without any caveat. We found evidence of citation manipulation via edited SCIgen bibliographies. This work reveals metric gaming up to the point of absurdity: fraudsters publish nonsensical algorithmically generated papers featuring genuine references. It stresses the need to screen papers for nonsense before peer‐review and chase citation manipulation in published papers. Overall, this is yet another illustration of the harmful effects of the pressure to publish or perish.ViewArtificial Intelligence and Machine Learning: Forging a New World for Scholarly Communication and the Advancement of ScienceArticle

A novel broadband wireless OFDMA scheme for downlink in cellular communications
April 2003
1 Junqiang Li
2 Kim Hojin
3 Yongsuk Lee
4 Yongsoo Kim
A new air interface scheme based on adaptive resource allocation method is proposed for downlink in cellular OFDM systems. The proposed method is featured as a novel technique for high spectral efficiency (i.e. frequency reuse factor approaching one) and high power efficiency. In order to reduce the co-channel interference (CCI) and improve the capacity in a cell, a cell is partitioned into three ... [Show full abstract] 120° sectors and three adjacent sectors from different cells are composed as a "virtual cell" which is centrally controlled. Assuming knowledge of instantaneous channel information for all sub-channels, bit rates and quality of service (QoS) requirements of all active users in base-stations (BSs), an adaptive resource allocations based wireless multi-user OFDM access is employed independently in each "virtual cell" by using the techniques such as multi-user diversity, interference measurement and bad channel avoidance over the whole system bandwidth. It is demonstrated that the system performance is greatly improved compared to the traditional systems with fixed channel allocation in cellular communications.Read moreConference Paper

A QoS-aware routing in SDN hybrid networks
December 2017 · Procedia Computer Science
1 Chienhung Lin
2 Kuochen Wang
3 Guocin Deng
Changing the whole network into a software defined network (SDN) is impractical due to high replacement cost. Therefore, there exist SDN hybrid networks, where SDN switches and legacy switches coexist at the same time. In this paper, we propose an SDN hybrid network architecture which can discover existence of legacy switches by using the Spanning Tree Protocol and thus have a global view of the ... [Show full abstract] SDN hybrid network. We also enable OpenFlow switches to cooperate with legacy switches by using the Learning Bridge Protocol without requiring any modification on legacy switches. By utilizing the characteristics of SDN, SDN applications can dynamically find routing paths according to pre-defined QoS requirements and current network status. We also propose a simulated annealing based QoS-aware routing (SAQR) algorithm which can adaptively adjust weights of delay, loss rate and bandwidth requirements in a cost function to find the best fit path according to QoS requirements. We evaluate the proposed SAQR in a simulated SDN hybrid network which runs applications with different QoS requirements. Simulation results show that the SAQR performs better than related work MINA in terms of the fitness ratios of delay, loss rate and bandwidth, with 88%, 90.8% and 86.5% of flows meeting their respective QoS requirements, in contrast to MINA, with only 63%, 82.4% and 87.5% of flows meeting their respective QoS requirements.View full-textArticle
